Learning the Influence of the Chemical Environment on Epoxy-Amine Cure Kinetics

Jessica Mangialetto, Robrecht René Verhelle, Niko Van den Brande, Guy Van Assche — 2026-10-10 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23285797

Accurate modelling of polymer-network formation requires describing reaction kinetics in a continuously evolving chemical environment. Epoxy-amine networks are a challenging model system: epoxy groups react successively with primary and secondary amines, while hydroxyl groups formed during cure promote autocatalysis. Semi-empirical models are easy to use, but always formulation-specific. Detailed mechanistic models provide a physically meaningful description by accounting for reactive and non-reactive complexes, but require numerous coupled reactions and parameters, limiting their extension to new formulations. Here, we develop a supervised learning framework for mechanistic model simplification. A physics-informed, fully connected residual neural Ordinary Differential Equation (ODE) network maps the instantaneous chemical state to two effective reaction-rate coefficients, reducing a detailed 13-step epoxy-amine reaction scheme to the two major reactions describing epoxy consumption by primary and secondary amines. The reduced model retains the corresponding mass-balance equations and Arrhenius temperature dependence, while the neural network learns how the evolving chemical environment modifies reactivity. Temperature and the instantaneous concentrations of epoxy, primary, secondary and tertiary amines, and hydroxyl and ether functionalities are projected into a latent representation and processed through stacked residual blocks to predict the two rate constants. These coefficients are then used within the kinetic equations to predict complete cure trajectories. The network therefore does not directly predict conversion, but learns the unresolved influence of the chemical environment on physically interpretable kinetic parameters. Training data are generated with the detailed mechanistic model across a broad range of temperatures, stoichiometric ratios, ether concentrations and conversions. Effective rate coefficients derived from the simulated concentration trajectories are used to train the neural network. Data are split at the stoichiometric-ratio level: all complete cure trajectories associated with a given ratio are assigned exclusively to the training, validation or test set, preventing leakage between correlated conversion points and trajectories. Generalisation is evaluated on unseen stoichiometries and processing conditions using both the predicted rate coefficients and the resulting integrated cure trajectories. Network architecture and training parameters are selected through automated hyperparameter optimisation. In a subsequent experimental stage, the framework is fine-tuned and validated using heat-flow measurements from differential scanning calorimetry and microcalorimetry, complemented by near-infrared spectroscopy to track reactive-group concentrations in real time. The reduced model captures hydroxyl-induced autocatalysis, differences between primary- and secondary-amine reactivity, and variations in effective reaction rates with temperature, stoichiometry and ether concentration. It thus compresses a detailed reaction scheme into a computationally efficient model while preserving a physically interpretable kinetic mechanistic scheme. This hybrid approach provides a foundation for compact and transferable models of polymerization kinetics, combining data-driven representation of chemical-environment effects with mechanistic reaction equations.

The effects of ICAP learning activities on learning outcomes

Christina Wekerle, Michael Sailer, Ingo Kollar, Tim Fütterer — 2026-10-10 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1016/j.learninstruc.2026.102482

Background The Interactive-Constructive-Active-Passive (ICAP) framework makes assumptions about the relationships between observable types of learning activities (passive, active, constructive, interactive), cognitive processes, and learning outcomes. Yet, rigorous empirical studies are still lacking establishing the validity of the ICAP framework. Aims This study investigates the effects of student engagement in passive, active, constructive and interactive learning activities on their surface and deep knowledge gain, and their motivation. Additionally, the study aims to determine the associations between students’ cognitive processes, their learning activities, as well as surface and deep knowledge gain. Sample Participants were 249 pre-service teachers. Methods After reading a text on multimedia learning, participants were asked to reread the text (passive condition), to underline central information in the text (active condition), to work on three open-ended tasks addressing the text (constructive condition), or to work on the same tasks in pairs (interactive condition). Results No statistically significant effects of condition on surface or deep knowledge could be detected. Regarding motivation, participants in the interactive condition reported statistically significantly higher levels of perceived relatedness compared to all other conditions. Furthermore, only participants in the interactive condition reported statistically significantly higher levels of interactive cognitive processes compared to those in all other conditions. Constructive cognitive processes were statistically significantly negatively associated with deep knowledge gain. Conclusion The findings suggest that the empirical foundation supporting the ICAP framework may not yet be sufficiently robust. Yet, more research is needed that applies a more rigorous methodology exploring the ICAP framework.

"Quantitative Modeling and Optimization of Radiation Dose in Routine CT Imaging: Balancing Image Quality and Dose Reduction under the ALARA Principle Framework"

— 2026-10-10 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.25258/ijddt.16.62s.35

Background: Computed tomography is indispensable in modern diagnostic imaging, but it is also a major contributor to cumulative medical exposure from ionizing radiation.Under the ALARA framework, dose optimization is not simple dose minimization; it is the controlled reduction of exposure while preserving the level of image quality required for the clinical task.International guidance identifies CTDIvol and DLP as the operational dose quantities for CT optimization and recommends using diagnostic reference levels as audit tools rather than patient-specific dose limits.Objective: To quantify the dose-image-quality trade off in routine CT imaging using the attached dataset and to identify which protocol pattern most closely satisfied an ALARA-consistent balance between dose reduction and preserved diagnostic quality.Methods: A retrospective observational reanalysis was performed on the attached Excel dataset comprising 40 routine CT examinations from a tertiary-care radiodiagnosis department.The dataset contained 14 head, 14 chest, and 12 abdomen CT examinations, divided evenly into Group A and Group B protocols.Group A represented a standard fixedparameter approach using 120 kVp, fixed mA, AEC off, pitch near 1.0, and filtered back projection.Group B represented an optimized approach using 80-100 kVp, AEC on, higher pitch, and iterative reconstruction.Outcomes included CTDIvol, DLP, effective dose, HU, noise, SNR, CNR, and an ordinal subjective image-quality score.Group comparisons used Welch's t test; exam-wise effects were explored with two-way ANOVA, Pearson correlation, and adjusted multivariable linear regression.Results: Overall, Group B reduced mean DLP by 43.1% and mean effective dose by 45.4% relative to Group A, with no statistically significant overall difference in noise, SNR, or CNR, although subjective score was modestly lower.In the head CT subgroup, Group B reduced CTDIvol from 50.8 ± 2.2 to 32.2 ± 2.4 mGy and effective dose from 1.579 ± 0.078 to 0.958 ± 0.065 mSv, both p < 0.001, without significant changes in noise, SNR, CNR, or subjective score.Chest and abdomen CT also showed large dose reductions, but CNR fell modestly in both regions.In adjusted modeling, Group B remained independently associated with a 2.57 mSv lower effective dose (95% CI, 1.86 to 3.28 mSv lower; p < 0.001).Conclusion: A lower-kVp, AEC-enabled, iterative-reconstruction protocol produced substantial dose savings across routine CT examinations.In this dataset, the optimized protocol was most clearly ALARA-concordant for head CT, where dose was reduced by about 39% without a statistically significant loss of objective or subjective image quality.For chest and abdomen CT, dose savings were still substantial, but additional tuning is needed to recover contrast performance while preserving the achieved reduction.

Spatiotemporal Geometry Integration Theory: Temporal Relational Closure as a Reproducible Higher-Order Signature of Conscious Neural Dynamics

Miroslav Svítek — 2026-10-10 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23287766

Spatiotemporal Geometry Integration Theory: Temporal Relational Closure as a Reproducible Higher-Order Signature of Conscious Neural Dynamics This manuscript introduces Spatiotemporal Geometry Integration Theory (SGIT), a mathematical and computational framework for investigating higher-order spatiotemporal organization of distributed neural activity associated with conscious processing. SGIT represents oriented relationships between EEG signals using discrete Hodge geometry, separating gradient and curl components of neural relational dynamics. Temporal dependencies across three successive analysis windows are described by third-order tensors. Their permutation-symmetry decomposition provides quantitative measures of higher-order organization, while Temporal Relational Closure (TRC) evaluates this organization against pair-preserving permutation-based null models. The manuscript presents experimental evidence from visual conscious-detection datasets, cross-cohort analyses, tactile perception, propofol-modulated brain states, and no-report perceptual paradigms. The findings indicate that higher-order relational neural dynamics contain reproducible information associated with conscious processing across several experimental conditions. The strength and specificity of the evidence vary across datasets, and some proposed mechanistic interpretations remain open to further testing. The framework provides explicit mathematical definitions, computational procedures, experimental protocols, statistical analyses, and falsifiable hypotheses for future research into the neural organization of consciousness. Software and reproducibility: The accompanying version-independent SGIT–TRC research repository contains reference implementations, frozen configurations, selected experimental results, and mathematical verification tools. Evaluating the performance of long short-term memory to other time series models for univariate and multivariate data

Chandra Prakash, Bibek Kumar, Abhinav Das Lodhi, Bijesh Dhyani, Chirag Joshi — 2026-10-10 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-026-73148-9

Abstract Time series data analysis is one of the difficult tasks in real-world data problems because the data entities are determined not only by physical causes, but also by the chronological sequence in which they occurred. In this work, we have examined how Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) model is perform well to traditional statistical methods and other machine learning models for performing univariate and multivariate time series single-step forecasting. We have also studied how memory cells and gated structures work in LSTM model with different cases to show advancement of LSTM as compared to other time series analysis tools. The key contributions of this work are methodological and empirical rather than architectural. Specifically, this study (i) provides a mechanistic, architecture-level interpretation of LSTM’s gating and memory dynamics that explains its temporal learning behavior, distinct from post-hoc explainable AI (XAI) feature-attribution techniques, and (ii) establishes a benchmarking framework that evaluates LSTM against statistical models, machine learning models, a forecasting-specific Transformer architecture (PatchTST), and a baseline (vanilla) Transformer under identical experimental settings on a real-world environmental dataset. Experimental results demonstrate that, within context-specific empirical evidence, the LSTM model exhibits more stable and consistent performance than the considered baselines. Cross-regional validation further shows that the observed performance trends remain largely consistent across both datasets, suggesting that the advantages of LSTM are not solely attributable to local characteristics of a single region. The findings are presented as context-specific empirical evidence rather than claims of universal model superiority and provide practical insights into forecasting under data-constrained environmental conditions.

The Language-Precedence Principle and the Conditions of Norm Formation: The Semantic Nucleus, the Layered Structure of Constitutional Review, and the Control of Arbitrariness

Yeon-Kyu Song — 2026-10-10 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.20434501

This paper establishes the Language-Precedence Principle: the semantic nucleus of language—the minimal identity structure that must be preserved for an expression to remain publicly re-identifiable as the same expression across repeated uses—is a logical precondition for the formation of any substantive public norm, and so precedes the norm itself. Public norms function as repeatable criteria: each norm ν maps its designated antecedent Aν to a range of permissible normative treatments (Nν: ext(Aν) → 𝒫(O)); this functional structure presupposes the public identifiability of the antecedent and the range, which a norm cannot ground entirely from within itself: internal definition must either presuppose prior meaning or fall into non-grounding circularity or infinite regress. The minimal identity structure securing this identifiability is the semantic nucleus. Its existence is established through structural analysis of norm formation, while the content of any given expression's nucleus is fixed empirically through a reproducible determination procedure. Three consequences follow. First, a nucleus's content is determined empirically through a community's repeated public linguistic use, not legislated from within the normative order. Second, when a constitutional provision invokes an expression, its direct object is not an empty signifier but the nucleus prevailing at the time of invocation, which functions as the supreme normative reference point within the constitutional order. Third, when a constitutional provision invokes a power-conferring expression, the normative capacity contained in its semantic nucleus is incorporated as well, so that a duty-performance right arises only with respect to the minimum institutional conditions necessary for the effective operation of that capacity and its constitutional placement. Building on this, the paper renders nucleus determination reproducible through a four-step procedure—reference community, evidentiary hierarchy, application of four criteria, and exclusion criteria—and reconstructs constitutional review as a hierarchy of four layers: semantic-nucleus power, placement power, duty-performance right, and legislative discretion, each with distinct grounds and degrees of rigidity. Failure to preserve the semantic nucleus—whether through deletion, substitution, or a nominal-retention form of infringement—triggers immediate unconstitutionality; placement and duty-performance infringements are reviewed under conditions specific to each; proportionality operates only within the discretion layer. The two general principles of review, protection of essential content and proportionality, are derived along separate chains of reasoning yet coherently unified through the functional structure, and apply across powers, rights, and duties alike, differing only in their mode of application; this is illustrated by two simulations: restrictions on the right to vote and statutory limits on the Board of Audit and Inspection. Drawing on Solum's fixation thesis, Greenberg's moral-impact theory, Alexy's proportionality theory, the Wittgenstein–Kripke rule-following discussion, and the formal-systems and coordination traditions, the present study identifies and grounds a condition of norm formation that, on the analysis developed here, the relevant works it examines generally rely on but do not formulate as an independent condition. It thereby reconstructs semantic fixation not as a starting premise but as a consequence derived from that condition. The main objections, including circularity, arbitrariness, and conflict with democracy, are examined to clarify the scope and limits of the theory. The deeper contribution lies beyond constitutional adjudication. On the analysis developed here, the Kantian, Rawlsian, Dworkinian, Razian, and Habermasian theories examined in this paper presuppose, insofar as they operate as public normative theories, that the expressions their norms employ must be publicly identifiable; yet they do not foreground that condition as an independent condition of norm formation. Where the meaning of legal expressions can be retroactively reconstructed, so can the normative standing of the individuals and institutions subject to them; rule-of-law control of arbitrariness is therefore not a moral postulate added to law but a condition of law's possibility, grounded in the conditions of norm formation, and respect for the individual finds protection in the semantic preconditions of normativity itself. The Language-Precedence Principle thus stands as a meta-foundation for substantive normative theory rather than as one normative theory among others. By grounding the control of arbitrariness in functional structure and formal foundation rather than intuition, essentialism, or moral postulates, the paper applies a method of mathematical jurisprudence to the meta-foundation of constitutional law and legal philosophy. The broader ontological foundation lies outside the scope of this paper's argument and is reserved for subsequent work. The semantic-nucleus theory is therefore not one more method of constitutional interpretation but an account of the linguistic precondition that interpretation, powers review, proportionality review, and rule-of-law reasoning must already presuppose in order to function as public criteria.

State-Conditional Divergence Principle (SCDP): A Testable General Systemic Framework for State-Dependent Transitions Between Opposing Functional Regimes

Shamiul Hoque Shan — 2026-10-10 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23285209

AbstractThis paper proposes the State-Conditional Divergence Principle (SCDP), a testable generalsystemic principle describing how a system may transition between apparently opposing functionalregimes while remaining the same system. The central claim is not that every system must exhibitsuch transitions, but that when multiple objectives, constraints, environmental inputs, and statevariables jointly alter the relative utility of available actions, a regime reversal can arise withoutrequiring a change in system identity.The framework was generated from a primary psychological case involving an apparent approach-withdrawal contradiction in attachment-related behavior. The primary case is treated as amechanism-generating observation rather than as proof of a universal law or as a clinical diagnosis.Attachment research provides empirical support for the existence of state-sensitive emotion-regulation and relationship processes, while also demonstrating that the expression of attachmentorientations depends on contextual conditions [1, 2, 4]. The generalized theory deliberately removesattachment-specific variables from its core mathematical formulation.SCDP is represented using a state-space architecture in which a system state x_{t}, objectivevector G_{t}, constraint vector C_{t}, environmental input e_{t}, and implementation/evaluation mediumM_{t} determine a relative action value. For two competing regimes, the sign of \Delta J_{t}=J_{A,t}-J_{B,i}determines the currently preferred regime. A state transition that changes the sign of this quantityproduces a regime transition A\rightarrow B without requiring a change of system identity.The paper separates observation, interpretation, formal model, prediction, simulation, and empir-ical validation. Thirty independent cross-domain validation cases are organized across philosophical-epistemological, psychological-neurocognitive, and systems cybernetic-mathematical layers. Syn-thetic simulations are explicitly classified as synthetic and are not treated as empirical confirmation.Null cases, counterexamples, parameter sensitivity, identifiability, boundary conditions, and falsifia-bility criteria are specified.The present status of SCDP is therefore a testable general systemic principle, not an establisheduniversal law.

Analysis code, datasets, and results accompanying the manuscript "Scale degeneracy: the physical basis of calibration and absolute quantification in molecular binding" (preprint: ChemRxiv, DOI: 10.26434/chemrxiv-2024-19rj6). Source repository: https://github.com/lihuanlz/TCS_v4.0 The TCS framework derives the exact equation of state for equilibrium finite-site binding from the canonical partition function, reducing every binding-based assay to two dimensionless quantities: the normalized target feed (xi) and the binding-regime parameter (kappa). Immunofluorescence and IL-6 measurements were performed by the authors; literature datasets were digitised or reanalysed from the cited publications. Machine-verified proofs: all 76 numbered mathematical statements in the manuscript and Supplementary Information are formalized in Lean 4 (lean_tcs/ in the repository: partition-function factorization, scale group, analog and digital statistics, Fisher identifiability, scale degeneracy, and platform limits), compile with no unproven steps, and are re-verified by CI on every commit. The Lean project is not executed inside this capsule, because reproducible runs have no network access to provision the Lean toolchain and mathlib cache.

Conditional Identifiability and Inference for OFT-Derived VGM Measurement Predictions: Nuisance Geometry, Calibration, and an Exact Gaussian Benchmark

Hasan Sigergok — 2026-10-10 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23286528

This paper develops a conditional inverse-analysis layer for OFT/VGM measurement predictions, following the forward-response framework of VGM XIV. It distinguishes equality of observation laws from equality of mean readouts, joint-parameter equivalence from nuisance-family overlap, and structural identification from finite-noise resolution. Efficient score projection and the Fisher Schur complement quantify information retained after nuisance adjustment under stated regularity assumptions. An exact Gaussian continuation of the two-channel measurement benchmark derives the identifiable amplitude, its profile likelihood and sampling variance, the loss of common-mode identification, and the recovery supplied by an independent calibration observation. A proper Gaussian prior yields an explicit posterior that remains equal to the prior when the response vanishes. An exact one-sided boundary-null calculation illustrates why ordinary chi-square calibration cannot be applied indiscriminately. Frequentist and Bayesian constructions are kept distinct, with conditional nuisance laws, uncertainty dependence, and reproducible pre-test choices made explicit. All formulas describe a declared idealized model; no empirical dataset, fitted parameter, detection significance, or microscopic OFT realization is supplied. The release includes symbolic verification and defines the additional products required before a genuinely withheld empirical test.

Authority-Preserving Adaptive Scientific Systems A Proposed Research Direction for Trustworthy, Reproducible, and Adaptive Scientific Discovery

Egidijus Kasiulevičius, Azuolas Kasiulevicius, Saule Kasiuleviciute, Ausra Kasiuleviciene — 2026-10-10 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23272764

Authority-Preserving Adaptive Scientific Systems A Proposed Research Direction for Trustworthy, Reproducible, and Adaptive Scientific Discovery Authors: Egidijus Kasiulevičius, Ąžuolas Kasiulevičius, Saulė Kasiulevičiūtė, Aušra Kasiulevičienė Version: 1.0Date: 10 October 2026Record type: Research concept / Research proposalLicense: Creative Commons Attribution–NonCommercial–NoDerivatives 4.0 International (CC BY-NC-ND 4.0)Project architecture: AUŠRA — EGIDA–LASO-Σ–AEE–KASK-Q–BITE Abstract This paper proposes Authority-Preserving Adaptive Scientific Systems (APASS) as a research direction concerned with how adaptive computational systems can support scientific discovery while preserving evidence provenance, conditional authorization, structural validity, reproducibility, and proportional action. The proposal is motivated by a coordination problem: scientific AI systems may retrieve information, generate hypotheses, update internal memory, detect changes, and recommend actions, yet these capabilities do not by themselves guarantee that the evidence used is appropriate, that conclusions are traceable, or that adaptation remains within justified boundaries. The AUŠRA architecture offers a modular framework for investigating this problem. EGIDA provides structured information addressing; LASO-Σ evaluates conditional memory authority; AEE explores controlled adaptation; KASK-Q evaluates structural regime changes; and BITE coordinates proportional action and feedback. The proposed research question is whether their integration produces measurable benefits over appropriately configured baseline systems. This paper establishes a conceptual framework and a falsifiable research programme, not a claim that a new scientific discipline has already been established or that the proposed advantages have been experimentally demonstrated. Validation requires documented implementations, fair comparative experiments, ablation studies, uncertainty estimates, and independent replication. Keywords: authority-preserving adaptation; scientific AI; reproducible research; evidence provenance; adaptive systems; conditional memory authority; research orchestration; AUŠRA; EGIDA; LASO-Σ; KASK-Q; BITE. 1. Research motivation Scientific discovery increasingly involves complex computational workflows: literature retrieval, evidence assessment, hypothesis generation, experimental design, analysis, interpretation, and replication. Automating individual steps does not automatically guarantee the reliability of the entire process. APASS proposes studying a central question: How can a scientific system adapt and improve without losing the provenance, authorization boundaries, and reproducibility needed to justify its conclusions and actions? The proposed direction connects several established research areas while focusing on their integration into one evaluable workflow. Its novelty remains a hypothesis to be assessed against existing literature and experimental baselines. 2. Proposed architecture The AUŠRA framework separates complementary functions: EGIDA — Information addressing: identifies where relevant information can be accessed and aims to reduce unnecessary retrieval work. LASO-Σ — Conditional memory authority: evaluates whether stored information should influence a current decision, considering relevance, uncertainty, risk, context, and provenance. AEE — Controlled adaptation: investigates bounded perturbations and their effects on learning, recovery, and performance. KASK-Q — Structural qualification: evaluates whether observed changes reflect meaningful regime changes rather than noise or misleading signals. BITE — Proportional action and feedback: coordinates responses and resource allocation according to the available evidence, constraints, and system state. The intended cycle is: Environment → information access → authority assessment → controlled adaptation → structural qualification → proportional action → evaluation and feedback → updated system state. The modules are proposed as complementary functions, not interchangeable components. The value of the integrated architecture must be tested rather than assumed. 3. What could improve in scientific research? If validated, APASS could support: More traceable literature reviews: linking research claims to identifiable sources and recording the basis for their use. Better hypothesis management: distinguishing established observations, interpretations, uncertain hypotheses, and rejected explanations. More informative negative results: preserving failed approaches and the conditions under which they failed. More reproducible computational workflows: recording versions, parameters, data provenance, decisions, and experimental outputs. More disciplined adaptation: testing new approaches within defined constraints and measuring recovery after failure. More efficient resource allocation: directing computation toward experiments with greater expected informational or scientific value. These are potential benefits, not established outcomes. Each requires a separate operational definition and empirical test. 4. Core research principles The proposed programme adopts five distinctions: Information availability is not the same as permission to use it. Evidence quality is not the same as the correctness of an interpretation. Detecting a change is not the same as identifying its cause. Improving a task score is not sufficient evidence of scientific reliability. Component-level success does not guarantee system-level success. These distinctions define the questions that the integrated architecture should help investigate. 5. Proposed research programme R1 — Information retrieval Compare EGIDA-based addressing with suitable conventional retrieval methods under the same datasets, hardware, resource budgets, and query requirements. Metrics: retrieval correctness, recall, candidate count, latency, memory, computational cost, and index maintenance overhead. R2 — Evidence and memory authority Test whether conditional authority mechanisms reduce the use of irrelevant, misleading, stale, or contradictory information without suppressing useful evidence. Metrics: decision accuracy, inappropriate influence rate, useful-evidence retention, provenance completeness, and recovery after misleading inputs. R3 — Controlled adaptation Compare no perturbation, random perturbation, controlled subcritical perturbation, and overcritical perturbation. Metrics: performance change, adaptation rate, recovery time, failure rate, constraint violations, and retained performance after environmental change. R4 — Structural qualification Evaluate whether KASK-Q distinguishes genuine regime changes from noise, smooth variation, and misleading correlations. Metrics: sensitivity, specificity, false-positive rate, detection delay, localization error, calibration, and uncertainty. R5 — Integrated architecture Compare appropriately configured baseline systems with EGIDA+AEE and the complete AUŠRA pipeline. Use component ablations to establish whether any improvement is attributable to individual modules or their interactions. Report results across multiple seeds and environments, including confidence intervals and negative outcomes. R6 — Scientific workflow reproducibility Evaluate whether the architecture improves the ability of an independent researcher to reconstruct the path from a question to its sources, hypothesis, experimental procedure, results, and conclusion. Metrics: successful reproduction rate, missing-provenance rate, reconstruction time, unsupported-claim rate, and total computational cost. 6. Evaluation framework A proposed multidimensional evaluation vector is: [Q_{\mathrm{APASS}}=(I,A,S,R,C)] where: (I) = information access quality; (A) = preservation of evidence and authorization boundaries; (S) = structural-change identification quality; (R) = adaptation, recovery, and reproducibility; (C) = computational, memory, and energy cost. This vector is a proposed evaluation framework, not a validated universal score. Each dimension must be operationalized, measured separately, and reported with its uncertainty. A single aggregate score should not conceal safety failures or trade-offs. 7. Falsifiability and success criteria The central hypothesis is that a carefully integrated authority-preserving adaptive architecture can improve scientific workflow reliability or efficiency over fair baselines without unacceptable losses in accuracy, reproducibility, or constraint compliance. The hypothesis would be weakened or rejected if: the integrated system does not outperform suitable baselines on preregistered primary outcomes; apparent gains disappear under fair resource matching; component ablations show no reliable contribution from the proposed integration; adaptation increases unsupported conclusions or constraint violations; provenance and reproducibility do not improve; independent teams cannot reproduce the claimed effects. Experiments should specify primary outcomes, baselines, exclusion rules, sample sizes, statistical methods, and stopping criteria before results are examined. 8. Current evidence status and limitations This document defines a research direction and experimental programme. It does not report a completed, independently replicated APASS benchmark. Previously described AUŠRA and component-level prototype results should be treated as project-reported evidence until the corresponding code, datasets, protocols, logs, and analysis are available for inspection and independent reproduction. Planned experiments must not be presented as completed experiments. The proposal also does not establish that all its component ideas are unprecedented. A systematic literature review is required to establish the precise contribution relative to resear

Microservice migrations often produce the opposite of their promise: a distributed monolith that carries the cost of distribution without its benefits. Treating the metric as a gaming target, we give a threat model, adversarial analysis (G1-G14), and conformance requirements (N1-N23). Darwin Gating for Auditable Reduced-Formula Generation from Statistical-Balance Models

Guojun Pan — 2026-10-10 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23273007

Reduced formulas are often obtained by writing a plausible production–loss balance andfitting its parameters to a small experimental data set. This procedure is useful, but it cansilently mix dimensional inconsistency, unidentifiable parameters, empirical interpolation, andmechanism claims. We formulate a gated workflow that separates these tasks. A statisticalbalance layer proposes a finite family of reduced equations; a Darwin gate applies hard physicaland data constraints before any soft ranking; a held-out evidence gate decides whether a candidate is accepted, revised, or rejected. The resulting method is designed for high-viscositymaterials such as asphalt, bitumen, crystal-rich magma, and bubbly magma, but the contract isdomain-independent. We give dimensional, positivity, asymptotic, identifiability, leakage, andevidence gates; a finite-state audit; a generic structure-state balance; a gated constitutive law;and a validation protocol with abstention. The framework can output a concrete formula whenthe data pass all declared gates, while preserving an explicit boundary between a predictivereduced model and a demonstrated physical mechanism. In particular, a fitted state variablemay represent coarse-grained interfacial or flow structure without being called a vortex-fibrereconnection invariant unless independent observations establish that interpretation.

Conditional Measurement Response for OFT-Derived VGM Observable Fingerprints: Transfer, Calibration, Uncertainty, and an Exact Two-Channel Benchmark

Hasan Sigergok — 2026-10-10 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23285592

This paper formulates the forward measurement layer connecting a declared OFT/VGM theoretical observable to an instrument readout. It separates mathematical contrast, detector visibility, nuisance-robust separation, and statistical detection. Linear and nonlinear responses are treated with explicit validity domains, sampling conventions, calibration coupling, and joint uncertainty propagation. A weighted projection identifies local response directions that survive calibration and acquisition nuisance, while a Lipschitz bound supplies a sufficient finite-contrast separation criterion. An exact idealized two-channel benchmark exhibits response-rank loss, a calibration-degenerate signal, and a common-mode-free contrast whose bounded-error separation condition is derived analytically. Shared calibration and cross-covariances are retained in contrast uncertainty; geometric error sets are distinguished from probability laws. The benchmark is a reproducible mathematical example, not a characterized detector, a realized VGM-XIII source solution, or experimental evidence. The resulting prediction contract states what must be fixed before subsequent identifiability analysis and withheld testing. No empirical dataset, detection significance, or microscopic OFT realization is claimed.

What this is The Fragile Data Frontier (FDF): a framework for valid mean inference when a sample is too small to resolve distributional shape reliably. It separates three boundaries — finite-sample representability, statistical estimability, and inferential routability — and permits specialised routing only when all three are justified, otherwise profiling unresolved shape as nuisance. The deposit is the framework paper together with the machinery that produced it: every preregistration frozen before its producer existed, every producer with its captured stdout, every closure record with its md5 pins, a provenance gate, and a ledger declaring each pin whose artifact has since been rebuilt. The mathematical content Exact finite-sample geometry of the standardized shape coordinates under pinned conventions: sharp marginal ceilings, an exact extremal coupling of maximal skewness with the nearest support edge, and an exact demonstration that a kurtosis floor quoted independently of skewness is unsafe rather than merely loose. Theorem 8 (scale ancillarity). Separately standardized G₁, G₂, D are pathwise invariant under positive within-group rescaling, so their joint law does not depend on the relative scale ratio r. Hence TV = Hellinger = KL = 0 across r by identity, not by estimate — an exact automatic-failure condition for one gate, on a stated restriction. The invariance atlas: a channel × group table, every cell typed invariant / equivariant / not invariant, every non-invariant cell carrying an exhibited witness. The screen is one-way. Pathwise non-invariance refutes structural blindness; it does not establish that the channel's law moves. Cell (D, R) of the atlas is the counterexample: a real witness, and an exactly unmoved law under any symmetric parent. Theorem 9 (scale-ratio law separation). s_Y/s_X = r·R₀ with R₀ free of r, so distinct r induce distinct laws whenever 0 < R₀ < ∞ a.s. Nondegeneracy of R₀ is not required. This is point identifiability, not uniform quantitative separation. Theorem 10 (reflection identity) and Theorem 11 (log-location representation): the scale channel is a location family in log coordinates, and total variation depends on (r₁, r₂) only through r₂/r₁ for any R₀ law — general structure, not an F-law coincidence. Exact population total variation at the Normal benchmark. A monotone-likelihood-ratio argument proves the scaled densities cross exactly once; the crossing threshold is closed-form, so the supremum defining TV is attained: TV = 0.52468350291045 at r = 1 versus r = 2, n_x = 5, n_y = 7, confirmed by an independent numerical integration. Three fragility mechanisms — invariance, distributional, estimation — and the ladder that orders them: can the channel change? does its law change? can finite n resolve the difference? What the deposit contains 85 files: the manuscript in Markdown and Word; 10 preregistrations; 12 frozen artifacts; 17 closure records; 21 producers; 20 captured stdout logs; the superseded-hash ledger; the README. Every closure record quotes figures verbatim from captured stdout, never hand-populated. Every preregistrat

Return Theory (RT): Foundations presents the sector-independent mathematical foundation of Return Theory: a pre-physical architecture for observation by observers embedded in the reality they describe. Identifiability of Noise Sources in Bodily Judgments

Hongju Liu — 2026-10-10 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23279187

Changes in temporal response curves after brain stimulation do not by themselves locate the changing variability within a sensory or decision process. We examine this distinction in the public body-ownership and simultaneity experiment of D'Angelo et al. (2026; 30 participants, 12,600 binary judgments). Recomputing the released Bayesian causal-inference likelihoods reproduces all saved objectives within $2\times10^{-9}$. An information-criterion sign discrepancy is traced through the public code and all 30 source-data rows; correcting it preserves the aggregate preference for the noise-varying model. A parameter-free conditional test nevertheless rejects a mandatory zero-centered symmetry constraint of both released model families under their independent-binomial observation law (deviance 984.304; 19,999 conditional simulations; plus-one $p=.00005$). Adding task-specific centers improves prediction under a fixed five-fold within-cell partition; the noise-varying family with task-specific centers has lower held-out NLL than its prior-varying counterpart by 60.885 (mean participant difference −2.029; descriptive 95% interval −3.268 to −0.791). Building on established sensory/criterion-noise models, we construct a constant-sensory-variance, variable-criterion implementation that preserves every marginal probability of the released noise-varying observer, including its condition-specific threshold policy. All 1,260 fitted count-cell probabilities agree within $2.23\times10^{-16}$. We then derive a conditional repair: with calibrated marginal parameters, one shared internal sensory draw, and independent decision and lapse processes, one additional population joint-response probability identifies shared variance. Allowing bounded criterion correlation yields a sharp interval instead; near zero total correlation, centered observations have only second-order sensitivity. Exact binomial confidence-set projection propagates finite-sample uncertainty. All 240 prespecified valid synthetic scenarios meet nominal 95% coverage, while their often-wide intervals and 240 assumption-violation controls expose the practical limits. The contribution is a source-specific, reproducible separation of model comparison, model adequacy, and implementation identification, with an explicit additional-measurement contract. It does not identify an anatomical mediator or validate a theory of subjective experience. Methods preprint and retrospective secondary analysis of published bodily-judgment data, with conditional identification results and finite-sample model calculations. Reanalysis of existing human data is distinguished from new data collection and from prospective paired-report predictions. Substantial ChatGPT assistance under the author's direction is disclosed. Not peer reviewed. No new human or animal data were collected; no new pretrained language-model experiment is reported, and C1 is not claimed to be empirically validated. The CC BY 4.0 license applies to newly authored material to the extent rights are held. Included third-party materials retain their own rights and source-specific terms; no new blanket license is applied to the original data workbooks. The Mapping Inseparability (MI) Theorem in Zulfia Probability: Scope and Optogenetics Application

DR. ZULFIQAR ALI KHAN — 2026-10-10 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23283137

In Zulfia probability, a classical model (Ω, F, P) is extended by a binary admissibility switch∆ ∈ {0, 1} that records whether the experiment is released (∆ = 1, state A) or blocked (∆ = 0,state I). For an event η ⊆ Ω the resulting probability is PZ (η) = π P(η), where π = PZ (∆ = 1).We study the map (P(η), π) ↦ → PZ (η) and prove a Mapping Inseparability (MI) Theorem: theevent η and the switch ∆ are statistically dependent, with Cov(1η , ∆) = π(1 − π)P(η), exactlywhen 0 < π < 1 and P(η) > 0, and in that case neither factor can be recovered from PZ (η) alone.The inseparability is therefore conditional : it disappears in the boundary cases π ∈ {0, 1} andP(η) = 0. We show that observing ∆ restores identifiability of both factors, and we confirm theresults in a Monte Carlo experiment with a die. The paper is expository and makes no claim toreplace classical probability.

A Cross Sectional Study on Otitis Media among Children with Upper Respiratory Tract Infections in a Tertiary Care Hospital at Puducherry

— 2026-10-10 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.25258/ijddt.16.63s.150

Introduction: Upper respiratory tract infection is found to be one of the most common infectious diseases across the world.Otitis media is one of the complications of the upper respiratory tract infection, and it is the second most common disease encountered in the pediatric population.It is more commonly noted between 6 and 24 months of age.The risk associated with otitis media development depends on various host and environmental variables.Although upper respiratory tract infection is one of the major causes of acute otitis media, there are still not many studies examining its role in the disease.Aim: To study the prevalence of otitis media among children with URTI and their etiopathological factors in a tertiary care hospital in Puducherry.Methods: This cross-sectional study was conducted in the ENT department of a tertiary care centre in Puducherry.Patients with symptoms of the upper respiratory tract infection had been included in the study.After the initial assessment, all the patients were subjected to otoscopic examination and associated risk factors were assessed.All the patients were then assessed using the quality-of-life questionnaire.The statistical analysis was done using SPSS software.Results: A total of 255 patients with upper respiratory tract infections participated in our study.The most common age group affected by upper respiratory tract infection was 8 to 10 years, accounting for 38.4%.Male patients outnumbered female patients.The prevalence of otitis media among patients with upper respiratory tract infections was 41.9%.Among them, the acute otitis media was noted in 50.5%, otitis media with effusion was noted in 33.6%, and the chronic suppurative otitis media was noted in 15.9% of the patients.The study noted that age (p-<0.001),gender (p-<0.001),socioeconomic status (p-<0.001),locality (p-<0.001),exposure to smoke (p-<0.001),family size (p-<0.001),breast feeding (p-<0.001),formular feed (p-<0.001),consanguineous marriage (p-<0.001),history of atopy (p-<0.001) and family history of hearing loss (p-<0.001),sinusitis (p-<0.001),tonsillopharyngitis (p-<0.001) and the adenoid hypertrophy (p-<0.001)were found to be significant associated with the otitis media.And the study also noted a poor quality of life among the patients with otitis media (p <0.010). Conclusion:The study concludes that the early intervention of patients with upper respiratory tract infections who are at risk can help prevent the development of the disease.The study also recommends that adequate health hygiene measures and awareness about the disease should be created among school children and parents to prevent the development of the disease.

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1128/jvi.00848-26

The diversity within H1 and H3 subtype influenza A viruses (IAVs) in swine prevents effective vaccine control approaches with inactivated whole-virus vaccines. We addressed the challenge of controlling co-circulating hemagglutinin (HA) clades of swine IAV with the development of a multivalent messenger RNA (mRNA)-lipid nanoparticle (LNP) vaccine expressing eight HA proteins to maximize genetic coverage. We applied a computational approach to select eight HA genes that represented 95% of the observed IAVs detected in the United States between 2022 and 2025. Piglets were vaccinated and boosted intramuscularly with either individual HA mRNA-LNP or an 8-HA multivalent mRNA-LNP. Serum was collected to evaluate systemic antibody levels. Twenty-one days post-boost, pigs were challenged with a field-relevant H1 1A.3.3.3-c3 IAV strain. The 8-HA multivalent mRNA-LNP vaccine induced neutralizing antibodies against all eight antigens, and vaccinees were protected against lung lesions, with lesion scores similar to non-challenged animals. Homologous monovalent vaccination significantly reduced IAV detection in nasal secretions and in the lungs. Heterologous monovalent vaccination was not cross-protective but did not induce vaccine-associated enhanced respiratory disease. We provide evidence that monovalent and multivalent mRNA-LNP influenza vaccines elicited neutralizing antibody responses in pigs and protected against viral challenge. The versatility and capacity for rapidly updating the mRNA-LNP vaccine platform make it an appealing tool to improve animal health and minimize the circulation and diversity of IAV in swine.IMPORTANCEInfluenza A virus (IAV) is an important respiratory pathogen in swine, and zoonotic transmission of swine strains to humans remains a public health risk. Control strategies against IAV in swine herds rely heavily on biosecurity measures and vaccination. However, the antigenic diversity of IAV circulating in swine challenges current vaccination programs, and there is a need for broadly protective vaccines or platforms that can rapidly update components to reflect circulating diversity. mRNA-LNP vaccines have emerged as promising vaccine platforms, offering simultaneous delivery of multiple antigens, rapid development, scalable manufacturing, and potent immunogenicity. In this study, we assessed the immunogenicity and protective capacity of monovalent and multivalent mRNA-LNP vaccines encoding eight representative IAV hemagglutinin (HA) antigens. To our knowledge, this is the first study to objectively select multiple representative endemic swine IAV strains by quantifying genetic diversity within the phylogeny and to apply this selection to rationally design and evaluate a multivalent HA mRNA-based influenza vaccine in the swine model.

Child health service coverage estimation using routine health data from Ghana’s Eastern Region

Matthew Johnson, Winfred Dotse‐Gborgbortsi, Anthony Adofo Ofosu, C. Edson Utazi, Nuala M. McGrath — 2026-10-09 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1038/s43856-026-01946-3

Abstract Background Suboptimal resource allocation limits uptake of essential health services in sub-Saharan Africa. Reliable population denominators and coverage estimates are needed to better match health facility resources to local demand. An estimation approach utilising routine health data has previously been used at region-/district-level. This ecological study aimed to explore its use at lower administrative levels, taking Ghana as an example. Methods Data collected by Ghanaian health facilities from 2017-2020 were obtained from the District Health Information Management System and used to estimate childhood vaccination coverage and diarrhoea service utilisation in 2020 for the Eastern region, districts therein, and selected sub-districts and facilities. The estimation approach derived the denominator from routine data quantifying uptake of the first dose of the pentavalent vaccine and was calibrated and evaluated using household survey data. Results Here we show region-level coverage of 90.3% and 92.4% for second (Penta2) and third pentavalent doses, respectively, and 83.8% for the first dose of the measles-rubella vaccine. The variability and proportion of implausible (>100%) coverage estimates increase through lower levels; the Penta2 district-level median was 93.6% (IQR:87.3%-96.2%) and facility-level median 96.5% (IQR:92.0%-104.5%). At all levels, diarrhoea service utilisation estimates are lower than the survey-derived estimate; the district-level median was 7.7% (IQR:5.9%-9.6%) and facility-level median 2.3% (IQR:0.0%-5.5%). Conclusions Whilst this approach is useful for uncovering local variation in service uptake, integrating routine health data within geostatistical estimation approaches able to model the spatial/aspatial determinants of health-seeking may produce more accurate estimates, better informing facility-level resource allocation.

The Experience of the Incarcerated Population during the Pandemic

Audrey R. Chapman — 2026-10-09 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1017/9781009872447.007

Chapter 6 evaluates the impact of the pandemic on the incarcerated population. It shows how crowded conditions in prisons and jails in the United States and the absence of precautions and resources, often even the sufficient availability of such basic supplies as soap and running water, let alone personal protective equipment, resulted in prisoners experiencing an explosion of COVID-19 infections. Moreover, at the time of the pandemic, the country had one of the highest rates of people per capita incarcerated in the world. Inadequate prison medical systems also contributed to the problem as did the failure to provide sufficient testing and priority for the receipt of vaccines. Because isolation and quarantine were used as protective measures it increased the number of prisoners held in solitary conditions, some of them in physical spaces unfit for human habitation. Also, because the prison population in the United States is disproportionately constituted by people of color this treatment is one more indication of the structural racism of policymakers.

Background: Audible sound is increasingly recognised as a physical stimulus that may influence biological systems, including microorganisms.Previous studies have reported changes in bacterial growth and antibiotic susceptibility following exposure to audible sound; however, evidence regarding Qur'anic recitation and its effects on bacteria remains limited.This study compared the effects of Mozart's Symphony No. 40 and Qur'anic recitation of Surah Al-Fatihah on bacterial growth and antibiotic susceptibility.Methods: Escherichia coli ATCC 25922, Staphylococcus aureus ATCC 33591, and Bacillus subtilis ATCC 6633 were standardised to a 0.5 McFarland standard and investigated under three sound conditions: Mozart's Symphony No. 40, Qur'anic recitation of Surah Al-Fatihah, and a no-sound controls.Bacterial growth was monitored by measuring optical density at 600 nm (OD600) at 30-minute intervals for 6 hours.Antibiotic susceptibility was assessed using the disk diffusion 0.05.Results: Bacterial growth responses varied according to bacterial species, sound condition and incubation time.For E. coli, the Qur'anic recitation group recorded the highest OD600 of 0.786 at 4 recitation generally produced higher OD600 values during the early and middle stages of incubation, while the classical music group recorded the highest OD600 at 5.5 and 6 hours.One-way ANOVA demonstrated significant overall differences among sound exposure groups at the analysed growth time points (p < 0.001).Significant overall differences in inhibition zone measurements were also observed among exposure groups for all three bacterial species, with p values ranging from < 0.001 to 0.008.Conclusion: Exposure to Mozart's Symphony No. 40 and Qur'anic recitation of Surah Al-Fatihah susceptibility.Future research should incorporate controlled sound pressure levels and frequency characteristics, together with complementary measures of bacterial growth and viability, to improve reproducibility and further clarify the biological effects and potential of sound exposure.

Lateral flow immunoassay for highly sensitive antibody detection of Clostridium perfringens ε toxin

Binbin Wu, Jiaohu Liu, Hang Feng, Zehua Cui, Xiaoying Wang — 2026-10-09 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1186/s12917-026-05930-w

ε-toxin, secreted by Clostridium perfringens , causes severe enterotoxemia in sheep and goats, resulting in substantial economic losses in the small ruminant industry. Monitoring ε-toxin-specific antibody levels is essential for early disease surveillance, vaccine-induced immunity evaluation, and infection status assessment of unvaccinated animals. Current detection methods such as enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay are time-consuming and instrument-dependent, limiting their use in resource-limited settings. To address these limitations, we developed a gold nanoparticle (GNP)-based lateral flow immunoassay (LFIA) for the detection of ε-toxin-specific antibodies. The strip was constructed using Staphylococcal protein A-conjugated GNPs as the detection probe and purified ε-toxin antigen as the capture reagent on the test line. The assay enabled rapid detection of ε-toxin-specific antibodies with a visual limit of detection (vLOD) of 3.3 ng/mL. Quantitative analysis based on the calibration curve yielded a limit of detection (LOD) of 0.028 ng/mL (28 pg/mL). Furthermore, the results showed a strong correlation with those obtained using a commercial ELISA kit (R² = 0.93), confirming the reliability of the developed LFIA. Analysis of 84 clinical samples, including 20 samples below the LOD and 64 samples with concentrations ranging from 3.94 to 299.50 ng/mL, demonstrated the excellent practical applicability of the LFIA. The developed GNP-based LFIA represents a high-performance, rapid, and user-friendly detection platform for monitoring ε-toxin-specific antibody levels following clostridial vaccination, providing a powerful tool for epidemiological investigations of Clostridium perfringens .

Beyond antigen discovery: Rethinking Helicobacter pylori vaccination

Amin Talebi Bezmin Abadi — 2026-10-09 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1016/j.vacun.2026.500692

Despite over four decades of strenuous investigation, a Helicobacter pylori vaccine has not yet been successfully developed, licensed, or implemented in the marketplace. It is important to emphasize that vaccine development for H. pylori has been a public health imperative owing to its plethora of roles in several gastroduodenal pathologies. This unmet challenge has become rather stark in light of its omnipresent worldwide presence, classification as a Group I carcinogen, and ever-growing resistance against antibiotics-mediated eradication therapies (due to rising resistance, treatment failures, and reinfection). In this paper, we propose that the main impediment to the development of an effective vaccine is not the lack of novel antigens for investigation, but rather the lack of adequate understanding of the critical immunopathological requirements for lifelong gastric protection. Supporting this contention, experimental and clinical data suggest that an effective vaccine needs to trigger potent mucosal immunity and antigen-specific cell-mediated responses and generate long-term immunological memory that is capable of subverting H. pylori’ s sophisticated immune evasion mechanisms. We review the current paradigms for the use of precision vaccinology, computer-aided antigen identification, immunoinformatics, multi-epitope vaccine strategy, novel adjuvants, delivery vehicles such as nanoparticles and viral vectors, and the newest mRNA platforms to develop H. pylori vaccine candidates. Therefore, these technologies should be viewed as enabling platforms rather than substitutes for a deeper understanding of host–pathogen interactions, gastric mucosal immunity, and the mechanisms required for durable protection. Therefore, future attempts must integrate knowledge from immunology with rational vaccine engineering to produce successful vaccines to prevent and possibly even treat H. pylori infection, thereby limiting the global burden of H. pylori , antimicrobial resistance, and gastric cancer. A pesar de más de cuatro décadas de intensa investigación, todavía no se ha desarrollado, autorizado ni comercializado con éxito una vacuna contra Helicobacter pylori . Es importante destacar que el desarrollo de una vacuna contra H. pylori constituye una prioridad de salud pública debido a su implicación en numerosas patologías gastroduodenales. Este reto pendiente adquiere especial relevancia debido a su presencia generalizada en todo el mundo, su clasificación como carcinógeno del Grupo I y la creciente resistencia a los tratamientos de erradicación basados en antibióticos, como consecuencia del aumento de la resistencia, los fracasos terapéuticos y la reinfección. En este artículo proponemos que el principal obstáculo para el desarrollo de una vacuna eficaz no es la falta de nuevos antígenos que investigar, sino la comprensión insuficiente de los requisitos inmunopatológicos fundamentales necesarios para lograr una protección gástrica de por vida. En apoyo de esta hipótesis, los datos experimentales y clínicos indican que una vacuna eficaz debe inducir una potente inmunidad de las mucosas y respuestas celulares específicas frente a los antígenos, así como generar una memoria inmunológica a largo plazo capaz de contrarrestar los sofisticados mecanismos de evasión inmunitaria de H. pylori . Revisamos los paradigmas actuales relativos al uso de la vacunología de precisión, la identificación de antígenos asistida por ordenador, la inmunoinformática, las estrategias de vacunas multiepítopo, los nuevos adyuvantes, los sistemas de administración como las nanopartículas y los vectores virales, y las plataformas más recientes de ARNm para el desarrollo de vacunas candidatas contra H. pylori . Por tanto, estas tecnologías deben considerarse plataformas facilitadoras y no sustitutos de una comprensión más profunda de las interacciones entre el huésped y el patógeno, la inmunidad de la mucosa gástrica y los mecanismos necesarios para lograr una protección duradera. Los futuros esfuerzos deberán integrar los conocimientos de inmunología con un diseño racional de vacunas para desarrollar vacunas eficaces que permitan prevenir y, posiblemente, tratar la infección por H. pylori , reduciendo así la carga mundial asociada a H. pylori , la resistencia a los antimicrobianos y el cáncer gástrico.

A single-cell profile of the porcine immune repertoire: concurrent profiling of T cell receptor and B cell receptor V(D)J sequences in health

Sélectionné · envoyé par email

Hao Zhang, Qixin Zhang, He Zhang, Panfei Wang, Rui Geng — 2026-10-09 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1007/s44307-026-00139-2

Adaptive immune diversity is shaped by the complex organization of T cell receptor (TCR) and B cell receptor (BCR) repertoires across tissues. However, a systematic cross-tissue immune repertoire profiling in healthy pigs is still lacking. In this study, we used 3 14-day-old Duroc × Landrace × Yorkshire (DLY) crossbred piglets, all of which were male. After sample purification, a total of 71,731 cells were retained. We established single-cell profiling integrating paired BCR and TCR repertoires across 11 immune-associated tissues using single-cell RNA sequencing combined with V(D)J sequencing based on DLY crossbred piglets. Diverse populations of immune cells were identified, with significant tissue-specific heterogeneity being revealed in lymphatic, mucosal, and circulatory organs. Immune repertoire analysis demonstrated biased V(D)J gene usage, conserved complementarity-determining region 3 (CDR3) length distributions, preferential receptor pairing patterns, and extensive clonotype diversity. Cross-tissue analyses further revealed coordinated clonotype sharing and distinct clonal expansion patterns among immune cell subsets. In addition, using the Pig-specific V(D)J primers we designed, the sequencing results have achieved a modest improvement in the detection of immunoglobulin alleles compared to previous studies. It is expected to provide a more reliable technical solution for related research. Collectively, this work provides a comprehensive reference profiling of the healthy DLY crossbred piglet adaptive immune system and offers valuable resources for future studies of porcine immunology, infectious diseases, and vaccine responses.

Introduction Salmonella infection remains a pervasive diarrheal illness, particularly affecting populations in developing countries. The lack of an effective vaccine against non-typhoidal Salmonella is attributable to antigenic diversity among serotypes, incomplete understanding of protective immune mechanisms, and the difficulty in identifying conserved antigens capable of eliciting broad cross-protective immunity. Methods To identify conserved Salmonella antigens, we established a Salmonella -specific monoclonal antibody library using heat-killed bacteria. Single-Cell BCR and Transcriptome Analysis Revealed Potent Neutralizing Antibodies in Response to HAdV-4 Infection

Sélectionné · envoyé par email

Tingting Yu, Xiaolei Ye, Zheng Zhu, Wanrong Zhang, You Yang — 2026-10-09 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.3390/ijms27208952

Human adenovirus type 4 (HAdV-4) is a predominant serotype causing acute respiratory infections, for which no antibody drugs have been clinically approved to date, resulting in an urgent unmet medical need. This study aimed to identify highly potent neutralizing antibodies by dissecting the B cell immune response after infection, thereby laying a foundation for vaccine design and antiviral therapy. Peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs) were isolated from a convalescent patient who exhibited the most robust neutralizing antibody titers among 16 HAdV-4 convalescent volunteers. A total of 2710 B cells were isolated from the peripheral blood of this patient, and the characteristics of B cell subsets and B cell receptor (BCR) repertoires were comprehensively analyzed by integrating scRNA-seq and scVDJ-seq technologies. Candidate antibodies were subsequently selected for a series of functional and mechanistic validations based on the characteristics of BCRs and origination. Transcriptomic profiling classified memory B cells into three functionally heterogeneous subpopulations designated as MB sub1, sub2 and sub3. Combined with the characteristics of BCR, 92 selected antibodies from sub2 and sub3 were synthesized for further functional validation. Three potent neutralizing antibodies were successfully identified, among which 1QH7 demonstrated potentially potent virus neutralization capability in vitro and conferred robust in vivo protection in the Stat1-deficient mouse model. We further employed AlphaFold 3 to predict the complex structure of 1QH7 with the HAdV-4 Hexon protein and validated the key binding residues through site-directed mutagenesis. Additionally, the study elucidated the viral neutralization mechanism mediated by TRIM21. We established an efficient antibody screening strategy based on integration of scRNA-seq and scVDJ-seq and isolated a potent neutralizing antibody, 1QH7. This study lays a solid foundation for the rapid pathogen-bait-free, cell subset-guided screening antibody route for novel emerging infectious disease outbreaks.

Fluorescence labelling strategies for nucleic acids and current applications in studying RNA delivery

Alma F. E. Karlsson, Thomas J Adams, L. Marcus Wilhelmsson — 2026-10-09 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cbpa.2026.102784

Recent advances in nucleic acid delivery technologies have accelerated research in RNA therapeutics and vaccines across many areas of pre-clinical study. As the main limitation for RNA as a therapeutic remains its functional delivery to the cytosol, much current literature is focused on optimising the endosomal escape of internalised RNA molecules. Fluorescence labelling of RNA molecules offers an effective way to measure this experimentally, allowing researchers to develop assays which distinguish RNA location from protein production. In recent years, advances in fluorescent nucleic acid chemistry have produced a variety of novel labelling strategies. This work provides an overview of RNA labelling strategies, with an accompanying selection guide for researchers who design assays to measure RNA delivery. It also reviews recent applications of fluorescently labelled RNA in delivery studies and concludes with a perspective on how novel fluorescent label chemistries could be applied in future delivery-focused RNA research.

Novel Treatments of Cancer

Paraskevi A. Farazi — 2026-10-09 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1201/9781042002337-13

Cancer treatments have evolved over the years and have given hope to cancer patients by extending and improving their quality of life. The field is moving towards more personalized treatments with less toxicity. One of the areas that has been rapidly expanding in the recent years involves next-generation immunotherapies, including CAR-T cell therapies, TCR-T therapies, and bispecific antibodies. In the area of immunology, personalized neoantigen vaccines have also been developed and used for cancer treatment. Other therapies involve tumor microenvironment (TME) modulators, such as myeloid therapies and microbiome-based therapies. Gene editing of both immune and cancer cells has also been utilized for cancer treatment. Another type of cancer treatment involves targeted protein degradation, which has aided in designing therapeutic strategies against undruggable targets. RNA-based therapies, including siRNA, antisense oligonucleotides, and RNA-binding small molecules are also used for cancer treatment. Researchers have also taken advantage of synthetic biology approaches. Oncolytic viruses engineered to selectively infect and destroy cancer cells have been tested in several cancer types. Targeted radionuclide therapy, known as radiopharmaceuticals, is another type of cancer treatment and involves delivering radioactive isotopes directly to cancer cells. With increasing understanding of tumor metabolism changes and their importance for cancer survival, new therapies are being developed that target tumor metabolism.

Rabies immunoglobulin utilization and delayed presentation following category III rabies exposure: a 10-year retrospective study in Hangzhou, China

Jing Wang, Weiwei Cai, Jiaxin Chen, Jing Chen, Wanli Yang — 2026-10-09 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.3389/fpubh.2026.1916685

Background Rabies is an almost invariably fatal, vaccine-preventable zoonosis. Rabies immunoglobulin (RIG) is recommended for Category III exposures, yet real-world RIG utilization remains poorly characterized. This study examined temporal trends and factors associated with RIG utilization and delayed presentation among Category III exposure patients in China. Methods A retrospective study was conducted using clinical records of Category III exposure patients presenting to a high-volume rabies prevention clinic in Hangzhou, China, from 2015 to 2024. Calendar year 2018 was treated as a temporal reference year for descriptive comparison. Multivariable logistic regression identified factors associated with RIG non-administration and delayed presentation (>24 h post-exposure). Results A total of 14,270 eligible cases were analyzed. Overall RIG utilization was 37.54%, declining from 53.09% in 2015 to 7.14% in 2022. RIG non-administration was independently associated with male sex (OR 1.20, 95% CI 1.11–1.29), age 18–59 years (OR 3.25, 95% CI 2.95–3.59), exposure at home (OR 1.47, 95% CI 1.33–1.63), presentation in 2018 (OR 2.38, 95% CI 2.01–2.82) and in 2019–2024 (OR 4.49, 95% CI 3.96–5.09), and delayed presentation (OR 1.43, 95% CI 1.32–1.55). The overall delayed presentation rate was 34.47%; independent risk factors included age 18–59 years (OR 1.37, 95% CI 1.24–1.52), exposure at home (OR 1.57, 95% CI 1.41–1.74), and presentation in 2018 (OR 1.61, 95% CI 1.40–1.86) and in 2019–2024 (OR 1.77, 95% CI 1.62–1.94). Conclusion RIG utilization was low and showed marked temporal variation during 2015–2024, and one third of patients presented more than 24 h after exposure. Both outcomes were concentrated among working-age adults and domestic exposure settings. These findings describe service utilization patterns and identify subgroups in which gaps in PEP delivery are concentrated; targeted risk communication and routine monitoring of RIG utilization and presentation timeliness are needed.

Long-term immunoprophylaxis by TLR9 ligand mediates macrophage-ILC cross-talk against respiratory viral infection

Asuka Joy Tobuse, Kouji Kobiyama, Jun Tsuchida, Masamitsu N. Asaka, Daichi Utsumi — 2026-10-09 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1126/sciadv.aeh6480

Rapid development of host immunity is critical during the emergence of viral outbreaks when vaccines are not yet available. Here, we demonstrate that CpG oligodeoxynucleotide-schizophyllan complex (K3-SPG) induces antigen-independent, long-term protection against lethal respiratory virus infection. A single intranasal administration successfully enhanced host resistance to infection for as long as 100 days posttreatment. Mechanistically, we observed a lung-restricted biphasic innate activation of macrophages and innate lymphoid cells. Early protection was mediated by CD11b + CD11c + macrophages (DP MΦ) and monocyte-derived interstitial macrophages (IMΦ). Late protection was mediated by innate lymphoid cells that were epigenetically reprogrammed, suggesting that two distinct innate immune subsets contribute to the maintenance of disease tolerance in the lung microenvironment. Deficiency of TLR9 or TNF-α abrogated innate activation and protective immunity. Collectively, these findings demonstrate that adjuvant-induced prophylaxis is a promising approach to enhancing host resistance against respiratory virus infection, involving the biphasic cooperation between innate immune cells in the local tissue.
