(2001) – it remains singular at the zeros of – and that the smooth kink is instead a distinct degenerate branch of the underlying algebraic system. Finally, since the auxiliary equation (3) is biquadratic in , every family in Table 1 is shown (Section 6.3) to admit, alongside its Jacobi form, an equivalent closed-form Weierstrass-elliptic representation, with explicit invariants given directly in terms of and verified both symbolically and numerically (Appendix C) – a unifying result that a further attempted extension of this material tried to reach but could not establish rigorously, since its extra branches relied on undeclared parameters not tied to the governing algebraic system (12). We further resolve the case left open above: every family in Table 1 extends to a one-parameter family with a nonzero background free-surface level, obtained by replacing by in under the compatibility constraint (Theorem 1), verified to 50 digits against the original, unintegrated PDE system. The present work establishes exact traveling-wave solutions only; no claim of dynamical or spectral stability is made, and stability is left for a separate analysis (Section 9).

RI-SINDy: Regulation-Informed Sparse Model Discovery in Biological Gene Networks

Nicholas J Lucenti, Majid Bani-Yaghoub — 2026-10-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.64898/2026.10.04.756519

Abstract Oscillatory and saturating dynamics in regulatory gene networks govern processes such as cell cycle timing, circadian rhythms, and inflammatory signaling, and sparse regression methods such as SINDy offer an interpretable method for recovering their governing equations from time-series data. However, these networks are typically governed by Hill-type nonlinearities that become nearly collinear with polynomial candidates over most bounds of an oscillating curve, and placing them in a shared library produces ill-conditioned regressions and unreliable coefficient estimates. Standard approaches such as ridge regularization, library normalization, and orthogonalization can marginally improve conditioning but cannot remove the structural multicollinearity of these systems, in turn limiting our ability to produce accurate parameter estimates. To address this issue, we propose the Regulation-Informed Sparse Identification of Nonlinear Dynamics (RI-SINDy) method, which removes known regulatory terms from the regression and recovers their coefficients directly from an asymptotic drift-balance condition. Because the time averaged derivative of a bounded trajectory approaches zero, the coefficients of regulatory nonlinearities can be computed independently by constraining them to offset the mean contribution of the remaining terms. We demonstrate that this method effectively reduces library collinearity and improves conditioning numbers by 3–45 × , substantially improving coefficient recovery and regression accuracy. We test the RI-SINDy method on experimental Hes1 data and on synthetic Goodwin and NF- κ B models. On Hes1, it achieves the lowest trajectory error among the compared methods (Traditional SINDy, SR3, and Nullcline Reconstruction). On the synthetic systems, it recovers the correct sparse structure with lower trajectory error than SR3 and Nullcline Reconstruction, and in a Goodwin noise sweep it recovers all active terms at noise levels up to 15%, where Traditional SINDy and E-SINDy fail to. In conclusion, the RI-SINDy method provides a robust framework to overcome the issue of multicollinearity for system identification in biological systems.

Near-Exact Computation of Independent Chip Model Placement Probabilities for Thousands of Players

Wataru Inariba — 2026-10-05 — arxiv

DOI : —

The Independent Chip Model (ICM) converts the chip stacks of the players remaining in a poker tournament into finishing-place probabilities and prize equities. It is the standard model in tournament solvers, but its definition considers all possible finishing orders, and exact computation has been regarded as intractable for large fields. The same mathematics appears in other fields; for instance, the ICM is a Plackett-Luce ranking model with stacks as weights. We present DE-ICM, a deterministic algorithm that computes the placement probabilities of all $n$ players for all paid places in $O(M n^{2})$ time, where the number $M$ of quadrature nodes is fixed for the admissible inputs and the target accuracy. Its numerical scheme allows the target to be set close to the accuracy of double-precision arithmetic. The algorithm evaluates a classical integral representation in which, conditional on a player's exponential clock, the number of players ahead is Poisson-binomial. A double-exponential quadrature on a single grid evaluates every integral; its truncation errors have closed-form bounds, and the step size, which controls the discretization error, follows from the field size. A numerically stable deconvolution then recovers each player's leave-one-out coefficients in $O(n)$ time from one shared product. On exactly solvable instances with up to 4,000 players, the worst observed relative error of an equity is $4.0 \times 10^{-14}$ and the worst absolute error of a placement probability $1.1 \times 10^{-14}$. The full placement matrix for 1,000 players takes 0.2 seconds on one CPU core.

Data Fusion for Errors-in-Variables

Huali Zhao, Molei Liu, Tianying Wang — 2026-10-05 — arxiv

DOI : —

We study errors-in-variables problems in which a target study contains only a single error-prone surrogate of an unobserved exposure, while an external source study provides repeated surrogate measurements from a different population. The measurement error distribution is allowed to depend on the observed error-free variables, and the error-free variable distribution itself may differ between studies. We introduce a conditional transportability assumption that enables the use of external repeated measurements under source-target heterogeneity. Together with additional replicate-error conditions, it identifies the target conditional measurement-error distribution. Building on this identification result, we develop a data-fusion estimator for a broad class of target functionals. The estimator combines conditional deconvolution, flexible nuisance estimation, and orthogonal correction that reduces first-order sensitivity to nuisance estimation. For the proposed estimator, we develop a unified spectral theory covering both diffuse-spectrum and finite atomic-spectrum target functionals, derive a general asymptotic expansion, and establish consistency and target-specific convergence-rate bounds. The resulting convergence-rate bounds depend jointly on the spectral properties of the measurement error, the latent exposure, and the target functional. For finite atomic-spectrum targets, we further establish joint Gaussian and bootstrap limits, yielding inference for smooth moment transformations under an additional centering condition. In the reported simulations, Fuse-EIV has small bias for the primary exposure-related coefficient. Applications to the National Health and Nutrition Examination Survey illustrate how accounting for population heterogeneity and error heteroscedasticity can change empirical conclusions.

High School STEM Research as a Pathway to Scientific Engagement

Duli Pllana, Rudy Baez, Peter Drozd, Jennifer Donnelly, Chris Gottschalk — 2026-10-01 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.46328/ijses.8082

This study examines the association between early high school STEM research experiences and students’ interest in pursuing STEM-related careers. The research is based on an interdisciplinary, project-based collaboration between biology and mathematics teachers at a technology-focused urban high school in New Jersey, where students conducted a 21-day investigation of plant growth (beans, lettuce, and grass) under hydroponic and soil-based conditions, applying linear, exponential, and quadratic regression models to analyze quantitative data. In parallel, an anonymous survey was administered to students who had participated in summer STEM research programs (N = 36) to assess perceived impacts on career interest, critical thinking, and collaboration skills. Survey responses were analyzed using chi-square goodness-of-fit tests and effect size measures (Cohen’s w), and internal consistency was evaluated using Cronbach’s alpha. Results indicate that hydroponic systems produced faster, and more nonlinear growth patterns compared to soil-based conditions, providing an applied context for mathematical modeling. Survey findings revealed statistically significant, positively skewed responses across all measures (p < .001), indicating strong associations between research participation and increased interest in STEM, as well as perceived gains in critical thinking and problem-solving skills. These findings suggest that interdisciplinary, research-based learning environments may support student engagement and the development of scientific competencies at the high school level; however, results should be interpreted with caution due to the limited sample size and single-site design.

From bin to barn: A meta-analytical approach to mitigate methane emissions from agricultural by-products used in ruminant feeding

Soné Nell, Lobke Steyn, Mario Hasler, Hendrik P. J. Smit — 2026-10-01 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jclepro.2026.149633

Agro-industrial by-products (AIBPs) represent a sustainable feed alternative in ruminant nutrition, with the potential to mitigate enteric methane (CH 4 ) emissions. However, their CH 4 production potential has mostly been evaluated for one or a few by-products at a time, under differing in vitro conditions and reporting units, and, to the authors' knowledge, no quantitative synthesis has compared CH 4 production across a broad range of AIBPs. This study aimed to identify AIBPs associated with low and high CH 4 production to inform emission factor databases, CH 4 accounting and decisions on AIBP use. Scopus, PubMed, and CAB Abstracts were searched, with forward and backward citation tracking, for studies published up to 5 November 2024. A novel statistical framework was developed, reconstructing individual-level pseudo-data from published summary statistics and analysing them with a heteroscedastic generalized least squares model, to address heterogeneity and improve cross-study comparability. Of 516 records identified, 23 met the inclusion criteria, yielding data on 53 AIBPs from 14 countries across 5 continents. Mean CH 4 production across individual AIBPs was 23.1 mL CH 4 g −1 DM, ranging from 3.5 mL CH 4 g −1 DM for banana leaves to 72.8 mL CH 4 g −1 DM for citrus pulp. Group means ranged from 21.5 mL CH 4 g −1 DM for cereal- and grain by-products to 29.4 mL CH 4 g −1 DM for oilcakes, but no group differed significantly from the overall mean, whereas several individual AIBPs differed significantly from their group mean. Incorporating selected AIBPs into ruminant diets offers a viable CH 4 mitigation strategy within circular economy frameworks promoting environmental sustainability and waste valorisation.

Cerebral blood flow monitored during circulatory shock using hemodynamic trend indices: a diffuse correlation spectroscopy study

Randolph Seno Sinahon, Luis Miguel Gomero, Meltem İzzetoğlu, Patricia A. Shewokis, Shadi Malaeb — 2026-09-29 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cmpb.2026.109675

BACKGROUND AND OBJECTIVE: Reliable detection and monitoring of circulatory shock is critical to provide timely and effective treatments. Specifically, continuous cerebral hemodynamic monitoring is essential as cerebral tissues are vulnerable to hypoxic-ischemic conditions. Diffuse correlation spectroscopy (DCS) is a non-invasive optical technique that measures cerebral blood flow (CBF) in microvasculature by calculating a cerebral blood flow index (BFI). Current CBF monitoring by DCS commonly relies on BFI magnitude changes or changes relative to a baseline (rBFI) of optical signals. However, BFI and rBFI are often confounded by robust compensatory mechanisms meant to stabilize CBF, thereby masking potential early indicators of shock. To address this limitation, we applied a cumulative sum approach to develop BFI-derived Hemodynamic Trend Indices (HTIs), to selectively monitor dynamic trends in CBF leading to shock. METHODS: Newborn piglet models of controlled blood loss (BL) and hypoxia were selected to investigate BFI, rBFI, and HTIs response to shock. Ascending and descending HTIs (aHTI and dHTI) were calculated from two-sided cumulative sum of BFI slopes. Machine learning and statistical models evaluated these biomarkers' shock detection performance and sensitivity to insult progression. RESULTS: In hemorrhage and hypoxia groups, HTIs diverged from baseline at earlier insult epochs; HTIs at 10% BL (Hedges' g = -0.85, 1.13) versus BFI at 50% BL, and after only ∼10-30 min of hypoxia, while BFI and rBFI declined after 40 min. In No-Shock vs. Shock classification, dHTI outperformed absolute BFI, achieving ∼0.90 accuracy and >0.8 F1-scores. CONCLUSION: The proposed hemodynamic trend indices can monitor directional changes in CBF during insult progression towards shock, and may be further developed as potential clinical biomarkers for early indication of cerebral hemodynamic decompensation prior to circulatory collapse.
